Compressed and distributed least-squares regression: convergence rates with applications to Federated Learning
\name Constantin Philippenko, \name Aymeric Dieuleveut

Abstract
In this paper, we investigate the impact of compression on stochastic gradient algorithms for machine learning, a technique widely used in distributed and federated learning. We underline differences in terms of convergence rates between several unbiased compression operators, that all satisfy the same condition on their variance, thus going beyond the classical worst-case analysis. To do so, we focus on the case of least-squares regression (LSR) and analyze a general stochastic approximation algorithm for minimizing quadratic functions relying on a random field. We consider weak assumptions on the random field, tailored to the analysis (specifically, expected Hölder regularity), and on the noise covariance, enabling the analysis of various randomizing mechanisms, including compression. We then extend our results to the case of federated learning. More formally, we highlight the impact on the convergence of the covariance ℭ ania \mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}} of the a dditive n oise i nduced by the a lgorithm . We demonstrate despite the non-regularity of the stochastic field, that the limit variance term scales with Tr ⁡ ( ℭ ania ​ H F − 1 ) / K \mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)/K (where H F H_{F} is the Hessian of the optimization problem and K K the number of iterations) generalizing the rate for the vanilla LSR case where it is σ 2 ​ Tr ​ ( H F ​ H F − 1 ) / K = σ 2 ​ d / K \sigma^{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(H_{F}H_{F}^{-1}\right)/K=\sigma^{2}d/K ( Bach and Moulines 2013 ) . Then, we analyze the dependency of ℭ ania \mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}} on the compression strategy and ultimately its impact on convergence, first in the centralized case, then in two heterogeneous FL frameworks.



1
Introduction

Large-scale optimization
(
Bottou and Bousquet 2007
)
has become ubiquitous in today’s learning problems due to the incredible growth of data collection. It becomes computationally extremely hard to process a full dataset or even, to store it on a single device
(
Abadi et al. 2016
;
Seide and Agarwal 2016
;
Caldas et al. 2019
)
. This led practitioners to either process each observation only once in a streaming fashion and to design distributed algorithms. This paper is part of this line of work and considers in particular stochastic federated algorithms
(
Konečný et al. 2016
;
McMahan et al. 2017
)
that use a central server to orchestrate the training over a network of
N
in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
clients.

A well-identified challenge in this framework is the communication cost of the learning process
(
Seide et al. 2014
;
Chilimbi et al. 2014
;
Strom 2015
)
based on stochastic gradient algorithms. Indeed, iteratively exchanging gradient or model information between the local workers and the central server generates a huge computational and bandwidth bottleneck.
To reduce this communication cost, two strategies have been widely implemented and analyzed:
performing local updates
(
McMahan et al. 2017
;
Karimireddy et al. 2020
, see e.g. )
, or reducing the size of the exchanged messages by passing them through a compression operator, on the uplink channel
(
Seide et al. 2014
;
Alistarh et al. 2017
;
Alistarh et al. 2018
;
Mishchenko et al. 2019
;
Karimireddy et al. 2019
;
Wu et al. 2018
;
Horvath et al. 2022
;
Mishchenko et al. 2019
;
Li et al. 2020
;
Richtarik et al. 2021
)
, or on both uplink and downlink channels
(
Harrane et al. 2018
;
Tang et al. 2019
;
Liu et al. 2020
;
Zheng et al. 2019
;
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2020
;
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2021
;
Gorbunov et al. 2020b
;
Sattler et al. 2019
;
Fatkhullin et al. 2021
)
. These two strategies, although typically analyzed independently, are often combined. We focus on compression; to reduce the cost of exchanging a vector, three techniques are combined: (1) sending the message to only a few clients, (2) sending only a fraction of the coordinates, (3) sending low-precision updates.

Most analyses of the impact of compression schema rely on generic assumptions on the compression operator
\mathcal{C}
, typically either
contractive
, i.e. for any
z
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\|\mathcal{C}(z)-z\|<(1-\delta)\|z\|
with
\delta\in]0;1[

(
Seide et al. 2014
;
Stich et al. 2018
;
Karimireddy et al. 2019
;
Ivkin et al. 2019
;
Koloskova et al. 2019
;
Gorbunov et al. 2020b
;
Beznosikov et al. 2020
, almost surely or in expectation, see for instance)
, or unbiased with bounded variance increase, i.e., for any
z
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(z)]=z
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{C}(z)-z\|^{2}]\leq\omega\|z\|^{2}
for a parameter
\omega>1

(
Alistarh et al. 2017
;
Wu et al. 2018
;
Mishchenko et al. 2019
;
Chraibi et al. 2019
;
Gorbunov et al. 2020a
;
Reisizadeh et al. 2020
;
Horvath et al. 2022
;
Kovalev et al. 2021
;
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2020
;
Philippenko and Dieuleveut 2021
;
Haddadpour et al. 2021
;
Li and Richtárik 2021
;
Khirirat et al. 2018
, see among others)
. Unlike biased – and often deterministic – operators, unbiased operators typically benefit from a variance reduction proportional to the number of clients (e.g.,
Gorbunov et al. 2020b
vs
Horváth et al. 2019
).

In parallel, a line of work has thus focused on the design of compression schemes satisfying one of these two assumptions
(
Bernstein et al. 2018
;
Dai et al. 2019
;
Beznosikov et al. 2020
;
Horvath et al. 2022
;
Xu et al. 2020
;
Leconte et al. 2021
;
Gandikota et al. 2021
;
Ramezani-Kebrya et al. 2021
;
Horvath et al. 2022
)
.
Two fundamental strategies are typically combined: (1) quantization
(
Rabbat and Nowak 2005
;
Gersho and Gray 2012
;
Alistarh et al. 2018
)
, and (2) random projection
(
Vempala 2005
;
Rahimi and Recht 2008
;
Nesterov 2012
;
Nutini et al. 2015
)
.
These methods are compared based on (1) the number of bits required for storing or exchanging a
d
dimensional vector and (2) the resulting variance increase
\omega
or contractiveness constant
\delta
.
Consequently, convergence results are
worst-case
results over the class of compression operators: two compression operators satisfying the same variance assumption are regarded as producing the same convergence rate.

The goal of this paper is to provide an in-depth analysis of compression within a fundamental learning framework, namely least-squares regression
(
Legendre 1806
, LSR,)
,
in order to highlight the differences in convergence between several unbiased compression schemes having the
same
variance increase.

Especially, this analysis will highlight the impact of (1) the compression scheme’s regularity (Lipschitz in squared expectation or not) and of (2) the correlation between the compression of the different coordinates.
We highlight three examples of possible take-aways from our analysis, that will be detailed in
Section

3
.

Take-away 1
.

Quantization-based compression schemes do not have
Lipschitz in squared expectation
regularity but satisfy a Hölder condition. Because of that, their convergence is degraded, yet they asymptotically achieve a rate comparable to projection-based compressors, in which the limit covariance is similar.

Take-away 2
.

Rand-
h
and partial participation with probability
(h/d)
satisfy the same variance condition. Yet the convergence of compressed least mean squares algorithms for partial participation is more robust to ill-conditioned problems.

Take-away 3
.

The asymptotic convergence rate is expected to be at least as good for quantization than for sparsification or randomized coordinate selection,
if
the features are standardized. On the contrary, if the features are independent and the feature vector is normalized, then quantization is worse than sparsification or randomized coordinate selection.

We consider a random-design LSR framework and make the following assumption on the input-output pairs distribution

Model 1

(Federated case)
.

We consider
N
clients. Each client
i
in
[N]
accesses
K
in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
i.i.d. observations
(x_{k}^{i},y_{k}^{i})_{k\in[K]}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}^{\otimes K}
, such that there exists a well-defined client-dependent model
w_{*}^{i}
:

\displaystyle\forall k\in[K],\quad y_{k}^{i}=\left\langle x_{k}^{i},w_{*}^{i}\right\rangle+\varepsilon_{k}^{i},\qquad\text{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \varepsilon_{k}^{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})\penalty\ \,,

(1)

for an i.i.d. sequence
\left((\varepsilon_{k}^{i})_{k\in[K],i\in[N]}\right)
independent from
\left((x_{k}^{i})_{k\in[K],i\in[N]}\right)
.
We use the generic notation
(x^{i},y^{i},\varepsilon^{i})
for such an input-output-noise triplet on client
i
.
Moreover, we assume that the inputs’ second moment
1
1

1

In the following, we may refer to this matrix
H
as the
covariance
(in the case of centered features, covariance is equal to the second moment)
is bounded to define
\mathbb{E}[x^{i}\otimes x^{i}]=H_{i}
and
\mathbb{E}[\|{x^{i}}\|^{2}]=R^{2}_{i}
; such that
\mathbb{E}[\|{x^{i}}\|^{2}x^{i}\otimes x^{i}]\preccurlyeq R_{i}^{2}H_{i}
. For any
i\in[N]
, we consider the expected squared loss on client
i
of a model
w
as
F_{i}(w):=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(x^{i},y^{i})\sim\mathcal{D}_{i}}[(\left\langle x^{i},w\right\rangle-y^{i})^{2}]
.

Remark 1

(Almost surely bounded features)
.

In the case of linear compressors, we will also assume that for each client
i
in
[N]
, features are almost surely bounded by
R_{i}^{2}
.

This model is classical in the single worker case
(
Hsu et al. 2012
;
Bach and Moulines 2013
, e.g.)
:

Model 2

(Centralized case)
.

We consider
1
with
N=1
client. For simplicity, we then omit the
i
superscript.

We focus on the problem of minimizing the global expected risk
F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, thus finding the optimal model
w_{*}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that:

\displaystyle w_{*}=\argmin_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{F(w):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}F_{i}(w)\right\}\penalty\ \penalty\

(OPT)

Note that we assume that
\mathrm{Span}\{\mathrm{Supp}(x^{i}),i\in{[N]}\}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
to ensure the existence and uniqueness of
w_{*}
.

The empirical version of the risk minimization admits an explicit formula, yet is computationally too expensive to compute for large problems. This is why, in practice, LSR is solved using iterative stochastic algorithms, for example Stochastic Gradient Descent
(
Robbins and Monro 1951
, SGD, see)
. SGD for LSR is often referred to as the
Least Mean Squares
(LMS) algorithm
(
Bershad 1986
;
Macchi 1995
)
. Analysis of LMS
(
Györfi and Walk 1996
;
Bach and Moulines 2013
)
and its variants received a lot of interest over the last decades. Indeed despite its simplicity, LSR is a model of choice for practitioners because of its efficiency to train good and interpretable models
(see e.g.
Molnar 2018
, chapter 5.1)
. Moreover, its simplicity enables to isolate and analyze challenges faced in specific configurations, for instance, non-strong convexity
(
Bach and Moulines 2013
)
, interaction between acceleration and stochasticity
(
Dieuleveut et al. 2017
;
Jain et al. 2018a
;
Varre and Flammarion 2022
)
, non-uniform iterate averaging
(
Jain et al. 2018b
;
Neu and Rosasco 2018
;
Muecke et al. 2019
)
, infinite-dimensional frameworks
(
Dieuleveut and Bach 2016
)
, or over-parametrized regimes and double descent phenomena
(
Belkin et al. 2019
)
.

Our approach follows this line of work: our goal is to analyze the impact of
compression
in FL algorithms, by providing a careful study of compressed LMS, based on a fine-grained analysis of Stochastic Approximation (SA) under weak assumptions on the random field.
More precisely, we consider linear stochastic approximation recursion, to find a zero of the linear mean-field
\nabla F
.

Definition 1

(Linear Stochastic Approximation, LSA)
.

Let
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the initialization, the linear
2
2

2

While in LSA literature, both the mean-field
\nabla F
and the noise-field
(\xi_{k})
are linear, we do not here consider the noise fields to be linear.

stochastic approximation recursion is defined as:

w_{k}=w_{k-1}-\gamma\nabla F(w_{k-1})+\gamma\xi_{k}(w_{k-1}-w_{*}),\ \ k\in\mathbb{N},

(LSA)

where
\gamma>0
is the step-size and

(\xi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is a sequence of i.i.d. zero-centered random fields that characterizes the stochastic oracle on
\nabla F(\cdot)
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we denote
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma\left(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}\right)
, such that the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
is adapted to
(w_{k})_{k\geq 0}
.

We assume that
F
is quadratic, we denote
H_{F}
its Hessian,
R_{F}^{2}:=\mathrm{Tr}\left(H_{F}\right)
its trace and
\mu
its smallest eigenvalue. For any
k
in
\mathbb{N}
, with
\eta_{k}=w_{k}-w_{*}
, we get equivalently:

\eta_{k}=(\mathrm{I}-\gamma H_{F})\eta_{k-1}+\gamma\xi_{k}(\eta_{k-1}),\ \ k\in\mathbb{N}.

As underlined by
Bach and Moulines 2013
, (
LSA
) corresponds to a homogeneous Markov chain. A study of stochastic approximation using results and techniques from the Markov chain literature can be found for instance in
Freidlin and Wentzell 1998
or more recently in
Dieuleveut et al. 2020
.

(
LSA
) encompasses three examples of interest, the first one is the classical LMS algorithm.
Indeed, with the observations in
1
and
2
, for any client
i\in[N]
, any iteration
k
in
[K]
, any model
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\textsl{g}_{k}^{i}(w):=(\left\langle x_{k}^{i},w\right\rangle-y_{k}^{i})x_{k}^{i}

(2)

is an unbiased oracle of
\nabla F_{i}(w)
. This can be used to define the following three algorithms.

Algorithm 1

(LMS)
.

For LMS algorithm, with a single worker, we have for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
w_{k}=w_{k-1}-\gamma\textsl{g}_{k}(w_{k-1})=w_{k-1}-\gamma(\left\langle x_{k},w_{k-1}\right\rangle-y_{k})x_{k}
, thus equivalently

\xi_{k}(\cdot)=(x_{k}x_{k}^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[x_{1}x_{1}^{\top}])(\cdot)+(\left\langle w_{*},x_{k}\right\rangle-y_{k})x_{k}
.

Second, the case of a single client compressed LMS algorithm.

Algorithm 2

(Centralized compressed LMS)
.

A single client (
N=1
) observes at any step
k\in[K]
an oracle
\textsl{g}_{k}(\cdot)
on the gradient of the objective function
F
, and applies a random compression mechanism
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\cdot)
. Thus, for any step-size
\gamma>0
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the resulting sequence of iterates
(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies:
w_{k}=w_{k-1}-{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{k}(\textsl{g}_{k}(w_{k-1}))\,.

And finally, the extension to the distributed case.

Algorithm 3

(Distributed compressed LMS)
.

In our motivating example, each client
i\in[N]
observes at any step
k\in[K]
an oracle
\textsl{g}_{k}^{i}(\cdot)
on the gradient of the local objective function
F_{i}
, and applies a random compression mechanism
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{i}(\cdot)
. Thus, for any step-size
\gamma>0
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the resulting sequence of iterates
(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies:

w_{k}=w_{k-1}-\frac{\gamma}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{C}_{k}^{i}(\textsl{g}_{k}^{i}(w_{k-1}))\,
(we consider the randomization made on clients
(\mathcal{C}_{k}^{i}(\cdot))_{i\in\{1\cdots,N\}}
to be independent)

Remark 2
.

The analysis naturally covers any randomized postprocessing
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{i}(\cdot)
, beyond the compression case.

Challenges, contributions and structure of the paper.

Although there is abundant literature on the study of (
LSA
), the application to
2
and
3
poses novel challenges. Especially, most analyses of LSA
(
Blum 1954
;
Ljung 1977
;
Ljung and Söderström 1983
)
assume that the field
\xi_{k}
is linear
(
Konda and Tsitsiklis 2003
;
Benveniste et al. 2012
;
Leluc and Portier 2022
, i.e. for any
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \xi_{k}(z)-\xi_{k}(z^{\prime})=\xi_{k}(z-z^{\prime})
, see)
. More general non-asymptotic results on SA with a Lipschitz mean-field (i.e. SGD with a smooth objective) also assume that the noise-field is Lipschitz-in-squared-expectation i.e. for any
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{E}[\|\xi_{k}(z)-\xi_{k}(z^{\prime})\|^{2}]\leq C\|z-z^{\prime}\|^{2}

(
Moulines and Bach 2011
;
Bach 2014
;
Dieuleveut et al. 2020
;
Gadat and Panloup 2023
)
. One major specificity and bottleneck in the case of compression is the fact that the resulting field
does not
satisfy such an assumption.
The rest of the paper is thus organized as follows:

1.

In
Section

2
, we provide a non-asymptotic analysis of (
LSA
) under weak regularity assumptions of the noise field
(\xi_{k})_{k}
.
We show that the asymptotically dominant term depends on the covariance matrix
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
of the
a
dditive
n
oise
i
nduced by the
a
lgorithm
, as expected from the classical asymptotic literature
(
Polyak and Juditsky 1992
)
. The backbone results of our paper are
Theorems

1
and
2
which generalize the results from
Bach and Moulines 2013
for
1
. The limit convergence rate term scales with
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)/K
, which highlights the interaction between the Hessian of the optimization problem
H_{F}
, and the additive noise’s covariance
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
.

2.

In
Section

3
, we prove that assumptions made in
Section

2
are valid for
2
with classical compression schemes.
Although this single-client case is a simple configuration, it enables to describe the impact of the compressor choice on the dependency between the features’ covariance
H
(which is also the Hessian
H_{F}
of the optimization problem) and the additive noise’s covariance
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
.
Contrary to
1
, for which the noise is said to be
structured
, i.e. the additive noise’s covariance is proportional to the Hessian
H_{F}
, applying a random compression mechanism on the gradient breaks this structure.
This phenomenon is noteworthy: for an ill-conditioned
H_{F}
, it may lead to a drastic increase in
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)
and thus, to a degradation in convergence.
By calculating the additive noise’s covariance for various compression mechanisms, we identify differences that classical literature was unable to capture.

3.

In
Section

4
, we study the distributed
3
with heterogeneous clients.
We examine two different sources of heterogeneity for which we show that
Theorems

1
and
2
remain valid. First, the case of heterogeneous features’ covariances
(H_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
in
Section

4.1
; second, the case of heterogeneous local optimal points
(w_{*}^{i})_{i=1}^{N}
in
Section

4.2
.

These results are validated by numerical experiments which help to get an intuition of the underlying mechanisms. The code is provided on our GitHub repository.
We summarize hereafter the structure of the paper in
Figure

1
.

Notations.

We denote by
\preccurlyeq
the order between self-adjoint operators, i.e.,
A\preccurlyeq B
if and only if
B-A
is positive semi-definite (p.s.d.) and
A\mathrel{\mathop{\preccurlyeq}\limits^{\vbox to-0.90417pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vss}}}B
if
A\preccurlyeq B
and
A=B+O(\frac{1}{d})
. We denote by
A^{1/2}
the p.s.d. square root of any symmetric p.s.d. matrix
A
.
For two vectors
x,y
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Kronecker product is defined as
x\otimes y:=xy^{\top}
, the element-wise product is denoted as
x\odot y
, and the Euclidean norm is
\|x\|^{2}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}^{2}
. For any rectangular matrix
A
in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
s.t.
AA^{\top}
is inversible, we denote
A^{\dagger}:=A^{\top}(AA^{\top})^{-1}
the Moore–Penrose pseudo inverse. For
x,y
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
x\wedge y
for the minimum between two values, and
x\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\vbox to-0.60275pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vss}}}y
if
x\leq y
and
x=y+O(\frac{1}{d})
. For any sequence of vector
(x_{k})_{k\in\{0,\dots,K\}}
we denote
\overline{x}_{K-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}x_{k}/K
.
We use
e_{i}
to denote the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with zero everywhere except at coordinate
i
, and
\mathcal{O}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
the group of orthogonal matrices.
Finally,
all random variables are defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P}),\mathbb{E}
is the expectation associated with the probability
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra.
We define the set of probability distribution function
\mathcal{P}_{M}

whose second moment is equal to
M
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
:

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\{\text{probability distribution }p_{M}\text{\penalty\ over $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ s.t.},\mathbb{E}_{\varepsilon\sim p_{M}}[\varepsilon^{{\otimes 2}}]=M\}\,.

Any such distribution
p_{M}
is indexed with its matrix of covariance.

2
Non asymptotic convergence result for (
LSA
)

2.1
Definition of the additive noise’s covariance and assumptions on the random fields

For any
k
in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define the additive noise
\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{add}}
and the multiplicative noise
\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\cdot)
.

Definition 2

(Additive and multiplicative noise)
.

Under the setting of
Definition

1
, for any
k
in
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define:

\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{add}}:=\xi_{k}(0)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{mult}}:z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\xi_{k}(z)-\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{add}}\,.

Remark 3
.

Observe that
(\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{add}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is an i.i.d. sequence of random variables and
(\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{mult}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is an i.i.d. sequence of random field. The following assumptions, made for
k=1
, are thus equivalently valid for any
k\geq 1
.

Assumption 1

(Second moment)
.

{\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{add}}}
admits a second order moment. We note
\mathcal{A}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
s.t.
\mathbb{E}[\|\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{add}}\|^{2}]\leq\mathcal{A}
.

1
and
3
enable us to define the covariance of the
a
dditive
n
oise
i
nduced by the
a
lgorithm.

Definition 3

(Additive noise’s induced by the algorithm’s covariance.)
.

Under the setting of
Definition

1
, we define the
additive noise’s covariance
as the covariance of the additive noise:

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}=\mathbb{E}[\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{add}}\otimes\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{add}}]\,.

Secondly, we state our assumptions on the multiplicative part of the noise, especially its regularity around 0 (note that
\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{mult}}(0)=0
).

Assumption 2

(Second moment of the multiplicative noise)
.

There exist two constants
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}>0
such that, for any
\eta
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following hold:

A
asu:main:second_moment_noise.1:

\mathbb{E}[\|{\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\eta)}\|^{2}]\leq 2\mathcal{M}_{2}\|{H_{F}^{1/2}\eta}\|^{2}+{4\mathcal{A}\,}.

A
asu:main:second_moment_noise.2:

\mathbb{E}[\|{\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\eta)}\|^{2}]\leq\mathcal{M}_{1}\|H_{F}^{1/2}\eta\|+3\mathcal{M}_{2}\|H_{F}^{1/2}\eta\|^{2}.

The main originality of this section is the analysis under
Item

2.2
. This Hölder-type condition will appear naturally for compression in
Section

3
. Up to our knowledge, (
LSA
) has not been analyzed under this particular condition.

Under these assumptions, asymptotic results from
Polyak and Juditsky 1992
can be applied. Especially, we establish the asymptotic normality of
(\sqrt{K}\overline{\eta}_{K-1})_{K>0}
, with an asymptotic variance equal to
H_{F}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}
.

Proposition 1

(CLT for (
LSA
))
.

Under
1
and
2
, consider a sequence
(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
produced in the setting of
Definition

1
for a step-size
(\gamma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
s.t.
\gamma_{k}=k^{-\alpha}
,
\alpha\in]0,1[
. Then
(\sqrt{K}\overline{\eta}_{K-1})_{K>0}
is asymptotically normal and converge in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(0,H_{F}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1})
.

The proof of this result is almost straightforward and is recalled in
Section

A.4
. In the following, we establish non-asymptotic results in
Theorems

2
and
1
, that highlight the impact of
Item

2.2
.

2.2
Convergence rates for (
LSA
), general case

In this section, we present non-asymptotic convergence rates for (
LSA
) under the assumptions above. These results build upon the work of
Bach and Moulines 2013
. Our first result is the main result, under the Hölder assumption on the noise field, it is demonstrated in
Appendix

B
.

Theorem 1

(Non-linear multiplicative noise)
.

Under
2
and
1
, consider a sequence
(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
produced in the setting of
Definition

1
for a constant step-size
\gamma
such that
\gamma(R_{F}^{2}+2\mathcal{M}_{2})\leq 1/2
. Then for any horizon
K
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(\overline{w}_{K-1})-F(w_{*})]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2K}\Bigg(\frac{\displaystyle\|H_{F}^{-1/2}\eta_{0}\|}{\displaystyle\gamma\sqrt{K}}\wedge\frac{\|\eta_{0}\|}{\sqrt{\gamma}}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)}+\left(10\mathcal{A}\gamma\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\mathcal{M}_{1}\mu^{-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left(30\mathcal{A}\gamma\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{\mathcal{M}_{2}\mu^{-1}}\Bigg)^{2}\,.

The first two terms of the RHS correspond respectively to the impact of the initial condition
\eta_{0}
and the impact of the additive noise. The dependency on these two terms is similar to the one established in
Bach and Moulines 2013
in the case of LMS. Note that following
Defossez and Bach 2015
, we improve the dependency on the initial condition to
\frac{\|\eta_{0}\|^{2}}{\gamma K}\wedge\frac{\|H_{F}^{-1/2}\eta_{0}\|^{2}}{\gamma^{2}K^{2}}
. Regarding the noise term, the dependency on
\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)}{2K}
corresponds to the classical asymptotic noise term in CLT for Stochastic Approximation
(
Delyon 1996
;
Duflo 1997
;
Györfi and Walk 1996
, e.g., )
.
In fact, for a sequence of step sizes
\gamma_{t}
decreasing to zero, we recover the variance from
Proposition

1
.
Remark that in
(
Bach and Moulines 2013
)
and several follow up works, the algorithm under consideration is LMS (
1
, which enables to ensure that
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{2}H_{F}
: the variance term thus scales as
\sigma^{2}d/K
. On the contrary,
2
and
3
do not always satisfy
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\preccurlyeq\sigma^{2}H_{F}
: in such case,
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)
may scale as
1/\mu
.

The third and fourth term, that scale respectively as
\sqrt{\gamma}/K
and
\gamma/K
, are asymptotically negligible for
\gamma=o(1)
. Those term are proportional to the Hölder-regularity constants
\mathcal{M}_{1},\mathcal{M}_{2}
, and also increase with
\mu^{-1}
. The dominant term is
\frac{\mathcal{M}_{1}\sqrt{10\mathcal{A}\gamma}}{\mu K}
. Interestingly, when
\gamma
is constant (not decreasing with
K
), then the limit variance of the algorithm is affected.
Moreover, contrary to
(
Bach and Moulines 2013
)
, we do not recover a convergence rate independent of
\mu
. This dependency is un-avoidable as the multiplicative noise is only controlled around
w_{*}
: without strong-convexity, the iterates may not converge to
w_{*}
.
While these additional terms in the variance may be considered as a drawback, it can be mitigated by taking a step-size
\gamma
proportional to
1/K^{\alpha}
with
\alpha>0
small (
\gamma
is horizon dependent, but constant).

Corollary 1
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

1
,
with
\gamma=1/K^{\alpha}
, and
\alpha\in]0,1/2[
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(\overline{w}_{K-1})-F(w_{*})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{60}{K}\Bigg({\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)}+\frac{\displaystyle\|H_{F}^{-1/2}\eta_{0}\|^{2}}{\displaystyle K^{(1-2\alpha)}}+\frac{{\mathcal{M}_{1}}\sqrt{\mathcal{A}}}{\mu K^{\alpha/2}}+\frac{{\mathcal{M}_{2}}\mathcal{A}}{\mu K^{\alpha}}\Bigg)\,.

The decrease of the second order terms is then optimized for
\alpha=2/5
. To highlight the impact of the non-linearity in compression schemes, we provide for comparison the result for a linear multiplicative noise.

2.3
Convergence rates for (
LSA
), linear case

Alternatively, to cover the particular case of a linear multiplicative noise (e.g., to recover LMS or projection-based compressed LMS) we make the following stronger hypothesis:

Assumption 3
.

The multiplicative noise is linear i.e. there exists a random matrix
\Xi_{1}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
s.t. for any
\eta
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have a.s.
\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\eta)=\Xi_{1}\eta
. Moreover
\mathbb{E}[\|{\xi_{1}^{\mathrm{mult}}(\eta)}\|^{2}]\leq\mathcal{M}_{2}\|H_{F}^{1/2}\eta\|^{2}
.

Remark 4
.

Note that
\Xi_{1}
is not necessarily symmetric (in
3
and
2
, this results from the compression).

In addition to
3
, in the case of linear multiplicative noise, we also consider the following assumption.

Assumption 4
.

The following hold.

A
asu:main:baniac_lin.1:

There exists a constant
3
3

3

This letter
\Sha
is the Russian upper letter “sha”.

\Sha_{\mathrm{add}}>0
s.t.
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\preccurlyeq\Sha_{\mathrm{add}}H_{F}
.

A
asu:main:baniac_lin.2:

There exists a constant
\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}}>0
, such that
\mathbb{E}\left[\Xi_{1}\Xi_{1}^{\top}\right]\preccurlyeq\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}}H_{F}
.

Remark 5

(Link between
4
,
2
and
1
)
.

1
(resp.
2
) corresponds to an assumption on the second order moment of the additive noise (resp. multiplicative), while
Item

4.1
(resp.
Item

4.2
) is a (stronger) assumption on its covariance.

Theorem 2

(Linear multiplicative noise)
.

Under
1
,
3
and
4
, i.e., with a linear multiplicative noise. Consider a sequence
(w_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
produced in the setting of
Definition

1
, for a constant step-size
\gamma
such that
\gamma(R_{F}^{2}+\mathcal{M}_{2})\leq 1
and
4\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}}\gamma\leq 1
. Then for any horizon
K
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F(\overline{w}_{K-1})-F(w_{*})]\leq\frac{1}{2K}\left(\frac{\|\eta_{0}\|}{\sqrt{\gamma}}+\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)}+2\left(\gamma d\Sha_{\mathrm{add}}\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}}\right)^{1/2}\right)^{2}\,.

Theorem

2
generalizes Theorem 1 from
Bach and Moulines 2013
. It also highlights the impact of additive noise’s covariance, and the comparison between
Theorem

1
and
Theorem

2
shows the advantage of linear compression schemes. Indeed the variance scales as
K^{-1}(\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)+4\gamma d\Sha_{\mathrm{add}}\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}})
. As before, the first term
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)
corresponds to the asymptotic variance given in
Proposition

1
, and the second term is negligible: (i) for all
4\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}}\gamma\leq 1
it can be upper bounded by
d\Sha_{\mathrm{add}}
, and for LMS
(
Bach and Moulines 2013
, see)
, the variance term is
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)=d\sigma^{2}
, which is thus at least as large, (ii) it scales with
\gamma
thus is asymptotically negligible as
\gamma
tends to
0
.
Overall, depending on
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
, the algorithm may or may not suffer from the lack of strong-convexity (
\mu
tending to
0
).
More precisely, in the case of linear multiplicative noise, we can obtain a
O(K^{-1})
rate independent of
\mu
if and only if
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\preccurlyeq aH_{F}
, with
a
in
\mathbb{R}
. The proof of
Theorem

2
is given in
Appendix

C
, and follows the line of proof of
Bach and Moulines 2013
.

Conclusion:
we established rates for (
LSA
) for both the Hölder-noise case and the linear noise case. In the former, convergence requires strong convexity while in the latter, we can achieve
O(K^{-1})
for
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\preccurlyeq aH_{F}
. In both cases, the dominant term for an optimal choice of
\gamma
scales as
\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H_{F}^{-1}\right)}{K}
.

In the following section, we turn to the analysis of
2
: we show how the choice of the compression impacts both the linearity of the noise and the structure of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
.

3
Application to Algorithm
2
: compressed LSR on a single worker

In this section, we analyze
2
, i.e. compressed LSR.
In
Section

3.1
, we introduce the compression operators of interest and verify in
Section

3.2
that
Theorems

2
and
1
can be applied.
Then, in
Section

3.3
, we provide explicit formulas of
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H^{-1}\right)
for various compression schemes.
Finally, in
Section

3.4
, we validate our findings with numerical experiments.

3.1
Compression operators

Our analysis applies to most unbiased compression operators.

Definition 4

(Compression operators)
.

Let
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

1.

1
-quantization
is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}(z):=\|z\|\mathrm{sign}(z)\odot\chi\text{\penalty\ with\penalty\ }\chi\sim\otimes_{i=1}^{d}(\mathrm{Bern}(|z_{i}|/\|z\|_{2})).

2.

Stabilized
1
-quantization
is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sq}}(z):=U^{\top}\mathcal{C}_{q}(Uz)
, with
U\in\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{O}_{d})
.

3.

Rand-
h
is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h}(z):=\frac{d}{h}B(S)\odot z
with
S\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{P}_{h}([d]))
and
B(S)_{i}=\mathbb{1}_{i\in S}
.

4.

Sparsification
is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}(z):=\frac{1}{p}B\odot z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{\penalty\ with\penalty\ }B\sim\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\left(\mathrm{Bern}(p)\right)\,.

5.

Partial participation
is defined
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}(z):=\frac{b_{0}}{p}z
with
b_{0}\sim\mathrm{Bern}(p)
.

6.

Random Projection
, also referred to as
sketching
, is defined as
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(z):=\frac{1}{p}\Phi^{\dagger}\Phi z
, where
h\ll d\in\mathbb{N}
,
p=h/d
and
\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times d}
is a random projection matrix onto a lower-dimension space
(
Vempala 2005
;
Li et al. 2006
)
.
In the following, we consider Gaussian projection, where each element
i,j\in\llbracket 1,h\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
follows an independent zero-centered normal distribution.

We refer to the introduction for related work on compression. Operators
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sq}}
are quantization-based schemes while
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
are projection-based. Indeed sparsification can be seen as a random projection (for
h\ll d
,
p=h/d
and
h
randomly sampled coordinates
\mathcal{I}
from
\llbracket 1,d\rrbracket
such that for any
i\in\mathcal{I}
, the
i^{th}
lines of
\Phi
are equal to
e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and equal to zero otherwise).
For
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}
, the motivation is distributed settings, in which the intermittent availability of clients prevents them from systematically participating in the training. This can be modeled through
partial participation
: clients only participate in a fraction
p
of the training steps. In theoretical analyses, this can be handled as a compression scheme
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}
, in which the compression of a vector
z
is either
z/p
or 0. Observe that in the centralized case, this is slightly artificial as it actually means that no update is performed at most steps and that the step-size is scaled at the other steps.
Finally, we denote
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{I}_{d}}:z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto z
the operator that does not carry out any compression.

Remark 6
.

The analysis of random projection is related to Random features
(
Rahimi and Recht 2008
)
, usually used for Kernel learning in infinite dimensions. Nyström method
(
Kumar et al. 2009
, introduced by)
is another similar technique of compression often used in this setting, it consists of removing a subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\{1,\cdots,d\}
of lines and columns in the kernel matrix
K
.
Both techniques have been extensively studied in the context of linear and non-linear kernel learning
(
Rudi et al. 2015
;
Rudi et al. 2017
;
Rudi and Rosasco 2017
;
Lin and Rosasco 2017
)
.
Recently, the combination of SGD and random features has been analyzed by
Carratino et al. 2018
. However, their results cannot be directly applied to our setting for two reasons. Firstly, their analysis is for infinite dimensions, where they obtain a
O(1/\sqrt{K})
rate of convergence. Secondly, the compressions used in their approach are not independent at each iteration.

Remark 7
.

Diffusion LMS (i.e. distributed learning without a central server) has also been studied from the perspective of low-cost training by
Arablouei et al. 2015
;
Harrane et al. 2018
, but using only clients’ partial participation or sparsification. Contrary to our work they use biased compression and an adaptive correction step to compensate for the induced error. They provide results guarantying asymptotic convergence
(
Harrane et al. 2018
, see Equations (28)-(37))
.

3.2
Applicability of the results on (
LSA
) from Section
2

We first show that our results from
Section

2
can be applied for
2
with a random compression operator
\mathcal{C}
, in the case of
2
.

Lemma 1
.

For any compressor
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sq}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}\}
, there exists constants
\omega,\Omega\in\mathbb{R}^{*}_{+}
, such that the random operator
\mathcal{C}
satisfies the following properties for all
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

L.1:

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(z)]=z
and
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{C}(z)-z\|^{2}]\leq\omega\|z\|^{2}
(unbiasedness and variance relatively bounded),

L.2:

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathcal{C}(z)-\mathcal{C}(z^{\prime})\|^{2}]\leq\Omega\min(\|z\|,\|z^{\prime}\|)\|z-z^{\prime}\|+3(\omega+1)\|z-z^{\prime}\|^{2}\text{(Hölder-type bound),}

with
\omega=\sqrt{d}
and
\Omega=12\sqrt{d}
(resp.
\omega=(1-p)/p
and
\Omega=0
) for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sq}}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}
).

We note
\mathbb{C}
the set of unbiased compressors verifying
Lemma

1
.
Item

L.1
is frequently established in the literature and corresponds to the worst-case assumption, see the introduction for references. On the other hand,
Item

L.2
is the Hölder-type bound, which is not used in the literature up to our knowledge. The expected squared distance between the compression of two nearby points scales with the
non-squared
norm of the distance. Moreover, the distance is multiplied by an unavoidable coefficient scaling with
z,z^{\prime}
. Remark that in
Item

L.2
, we assume the compression randomness to be the same for the compression of
z
and
z^{\prime}
: formally, we control
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{C}(z),\mathcal{C}(z^{\prime}))^{2}
, with
\mathcal{W}_{2}
the Wassertein-
2
distance. This lemma is demonstrated in
Section

E.1
.

Remark 8
.

For a given
\omega
, note that the
communication cost

c
for quantization-based and projection-based compressors is not always equivalent. For
1
-quantization we have
c\approx\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{d}\log_{2}{d}+32
while for projection-based we have
c\approx 32\sqrt{d}
, for
\sqrt{d}
-quantization we have
c\approx 3d+32
while for projection-based we have
c=16d
.

Lemma

1
enables to show that
Theorems

2
and
1

2
are valid in the context of
2
.

Corollary 2
.

Consider
2
in the context of
2
, with a compressor
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sq}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},

\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}\}
. With
Lemma

1
above,
2
and
1
on the resulting random field
(\xi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
are valid, with in particular
H_{F}=H
,
R_{F}^{2}=R^{2}
,
\mathcal{A}=(\omega+1)R^{2}\sigma^{2}
,
\mathcal{M}_{2}=(\omega+1)R^{2}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}=\Omega R^{2}\sigma
. Therefore, it follows that
Theorem

1
holds.

Moreover for any linear compressor
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},

\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}\}
, under
Remark

1
, we also have that
3
and
4
are valid with
\Sha_{\mathrm{add}}=\sigma^{2}\Sha_{H}
and
\Sha_{\mathrm{mult}}=R^{2}\Sha_{H}
, with
\Sha_{H}
given below.
Therefore, it follows that
Theorem

2
holds.

Compressor

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h}

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}

\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}

\Sha_{H}

\frac{h-1}{p(d-1)}+(1-\frac{h-1}{d-1})\frac{\tau}{p}

1+\frac{(1-p)\tau}{p}

\frac{1}{p}

\frac{\alpha-\beta}{p}+\frac{\beta\tau}{p}

\Sha_{H}
(if
H
diagonal)

\frac{1}{p}

\frac{1}{p}

\frac{1}{p}

\frac{\alpha-\beta}{p}+\frac{\beta\tau}{p}

Where
p=h/d
,
\tau=\mathrm{Tr}\left(H\right)/\mu
, and for sketching
\alpha=\frac{h+2}{d+2}
and
\beta=\frac{d-h}{(d-1)(d+2)}
.

This corollary is proved in
Appendix

D
. We observe that a first difference in terms of convergence exists between quantization-based compression and projection-based: for the former,
only

Theorem

1
can be applied and the lower-order terms always have a
poorer dependency on
\mu
while for the latter,
Theorem

2
is applicable and lower-order terms do not necessarily depends on
\mu
. Indeed, the constants
\Sha_{H}
do not depend on
\mu
for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}
, and for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}
,
when
the features’ covariance
H
is diagonal. On the contrary, there is always a dependency on
\mu
for
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
, and for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}
when
H
is not diagonal.
In practice, this means that, among projection-based compressors, regarding lower-order terms, the convergence is expected to be slower for random Gaussian projection.

We now turn to the analysis of the impact of the choice of the compression on the dominant asymptotic term
\mathrm{Tr}\left(H_{F}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\right)
.

3.3
Impact of the compression on the additive noise covariance

In this section, we illustrate how distinct compressors lead to different covariances for the additive noise.
This shows how
\mathrm{Tr}\left(H_{F}^{-1}\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}\right)
is impacted by the choice of a compressor.

First recall that for
2
in the context of
2
, with any compressor
\mathcal{C}
, the additive noise writes for any
k\in[K]
, as:

\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{add}}\overset{\text{def.}\penalty\ \ref{def:add_mult_noise}}{=}\xi_{k}(0)\overset{\text{algo}\penalty\ \ref{ex:cent_comp_LMS}}{=}\nabla F(w_{*})-\mathcal{C}_{k}(\textsl{g}_{k}(w_{*}))\overset{\text{eq.}\penalty\ \ref{eq:def_oracle}}{=}-\mathcal{C}_{k}((\left\langle x_{k},w_{*}\right\rangle-y_{k})x_{k})\overset{\text{model}\penalty\ \ref{model:centralized}}{=}\mathcal{C}_{k}(\varepsilon_{k}x_{k})\,.

Also recall that
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
is defined as
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}:=\mathbb{E}[(\xi_{k}^{\mathrm{add}})^{\otimes 2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(\varepsilon_{k}x_{k})^{\otimes 2}]
. Moreover, note that
\mathcal{C}(\varepsilon_{k}x_{k})\overset{\text{a.s.}}{=}\varepsilon_{k}\mathcal{C}(x_{k})
for all operators under consideration (this is immediate for linear operators and results from the scaling for quantization-based ones). Consequently

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{k}^{2}\mathcal{C}(x_{k})^{\otimes 2}]=\sigma^{2}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(x_{k})^{\otimes 2}],

(3)

as
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{k}^{2}|x_{k}]=\sigma^{2}
. Ultimately, we have to study the covariance of
\mathcal{C}(x_{k})
, for
x_{k}
a random variable with second-moment
H
.

We thus generically study the covariance of
\mathcal{C}(E)
, for
E
a random vector with distribution
p_{M}
with second moment
4
4

4

Remark that we do not assume
\mathbb{E}[E]=0
. Indeed, all computations only depend on the
second-order moment

M
of
E
, not on its variance (and the convergence depends of the second
-order moment

H
of
x
, not its variance). It is clear, that
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(E)^{{\otimes 2}}]
does not depend on the fact that
E
is centered: indeed, for
R
a Rademacher
1/2
independent of
E
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(E)^{{\otimes 2}}]=\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(E)^{{\otimes 2}}]\overset{\perp}{=}\mathbb{E}[(R\mathcal{C}(E))^{{\otimes 2}}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(RE)^{{\otimes 2}}]
and
RE
is (1) centered (2) has the same second-moment as
E
. Remark that centering the covariates before learning does impact
H
: indeed
H=\mathbb{E}[(x)^{{\otimes 2}}]=\mathbb{E}[(x-\mathbb{E}[X])^{{\otimes 2}}]+(\mathbb{E}[X])^{\otimes 2}
). Centering subtracts
(\mathbb{E}[X])^{\otimes 2}
to the second moment, which is a rank-1 matrix, typically does not affect the smallest eigenvalue, but it can affect the top-eigenvalue.

\mathbb{E}[E^{\otimes 2}]=M
.

Definition 5

(Compressor’ covariance on
p_{M}
)
.

We define the following operator
\mathfrak{C}
which returns
the covariance of a random mechanism
\mathcal{C}
acting on a distribution
p_{M}\in\mathcal{P}_{M}
,

\mathfrak{C}:\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{C}\times\mathcal{P}_{M}&\to&\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\\
(\mathcal{C},\ \ p_{M})&\xmapsto{}&\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(E)^{{\otimes 2}}]\,,\end{array}

where
E\sim p_{M}
and the expectation is over the joint randomness of
\mathcal{C}
and
E
, which are considered independent, that is
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(E)^{{\otimes 2}}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}(e)^{{\otimes 2}}]\mathrm{d}p_{M}(e)
.

Figure 2
:
Illustration of Remark
9

Using a compressor
\mathcal{C}\in\mathbb{C}
, we therefore have by
Equation

3
:

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}=\sigma^{2}\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},p_{H}),

(4)

where
p_{H}
is the marginal distribution of
x_{k}
(for any
k
).

Remark 9

(Dependence on
p_{M}
, not only
M
)
.

Note that, for
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}
, there exist two distributions
p_{M},p^{\prime}_{M}
with the same covariance
M
, such that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},p_{M})\neq\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},p^{\prime}_{M})
.
This is why we cannot simply denote
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},M)
.

Indeed, consider
d=2
and (1) a normal distribution
{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{1}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2}/2)
, vs (2) a
diamond
distribution
{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{2}}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\diamond}
, such that

\mathbb{P}_{\diamond}\{(1,0)\}=\mathbb{P}_{\diamond}\{(-1,0)\}=\mathbb{P}_{\diamond}\{(0,1)\}=\mathbb{P}_{\diamond}\{(0,-1)\}

=1/4\,,

and thus
\mathrm{Cov}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{1}}\right]=\mathrm{Cov}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{2}}\right]=I_{2}/2
. Then
\mathrm{Cov}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{1}}\right]\prec\mathrm{Cov}\left[{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}(E_{1})}\right]
, but
{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}(E_{2})}\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{2}}
thus
\mathrm{Cov}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{2}}\right]=\mathrm{Cov}\left[{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}(E_{2})}\right]
. We illustrate this on
Figure

2
: we represent
{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}E_{i}}
in blue and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}(E_{i})}
in orange for
i=1
(left) and
i=2
(right). We also represent the covariance matrices by plotting the ellipses
{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[E_{i}\right]}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}(E_{i})\right]}}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{M}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},x^{\top}M^{-1}x=4\}
(see
Definition

S1
)
5
5

5

The constant 4 is chosen so that for Gaussian distributions, the expected fraction of points within the ellipse is
86,4\%\simeq 1-F_{\chi^{2}(2)}(4)

.

We now compute for the compression operators, the value or an upper bound on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},p_{{H}})
.

Proposition 2

(Compression and covariance)
.

The following formulas hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{I}_{d}},p_{{M}})&=M\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{q},p_{{M}})&\preccurlyeq\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},M):=M+\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}\left(M\right)}\sqrt{\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)}-\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)\\
&\text{(with equality if $\|E\|$ is a.s. constant under $p_{M}$)}\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})&=M+{(1-p)}{p^{-1}}\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},p_{{M}})&=p^{-1}\left((\frac{h+1}{d+2}+\delta_{hd})M+\left(1-\frac{h-1}{d-1}\right)\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left(M\right)}{d+2}\mathrm{I}_{d}\right),\penalty\ \text{with }\delta_{hd}=\frac{h-1}{(d-1)(d+2)}=O(\frac{1}{d})\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},p_{{M}})&=p^{-1}\left(\frac{h-1}{d-1}M+\left(1-\frac{h-1}{d-1}\right)\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)\right)\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})&=p^{-1}M\,.\end{array}

Conclusion and interpretation.
Most compression operators induce
both
a
structured
noise
(
Flammarion and Bach 2015
)
which covariance scales with
H
and an
unstructured
noise, which covariance scales with
\mathrm{Diag}\left(H\right)
or
\mathrm{I}_{d}
– thus corresponding to an
isotropic
noise.

From the convergence standpoint, the asymptotic convergence rate scales with
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H^{-1}\right)=\sigma^{2}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},p_{{H}})H^{-1}\right)
. Therefore, the un-structured part in the noise is problematic as
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H^{-1})\right)
will strongly depends on the smallest eigenvalue
\mu
. This comes from the fact that the compression induces a significant noise in directions in which the Hessian curvature is very limited (thus directions onto which the contraction towards the optimum in the algorithm is weak).

A particular case is when
H
is diagonal (e.g. the features are
centered
and
independent
), we get the following corollary.

Corollary 3

(Compression and covariance, diagonal case)
.

If
M
is diagonal, then
Proposition

2
is simplified to the following (with the same
\delta_{hd}
):

\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{I}_{d}},p_{{M}})&=M&\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},p_{{M}})&=p^{-1}\left((\frac{h+1}{d+2}+\delta_{hd})M+(1-\frac{h-1}{d-1})\frac{\mathrm{Tr}\left(M\right)}{d+2}\mathrm{I}_{d}\right)\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{q},p_{{M}})&\preccurlyeq\sqrt{\mathrm{Tr}\left(M\right)}\sqrt{M}&\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},p_{{H}})&=p^{-1}M\\
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})&=p^{-1}M&\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})&=p^{-1}M.\\
\end{array}

Remark 10

(Composition of compressors)
.

For all compression schemes but
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}
, we observe that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C},p_{{M}})
is a function of
M
, which complements
Remark

9
. In that particular case, we can then denote
\mathfrak{C}(C,M)
. This means that the lemma can be extended to any composition of compression schemes, for example to compute

\mathfrak{C}({C_{1}\circ C_{2},M})=\mathfrak{C}({C_{1},\mathfrak{C}(C_{2},M)}).

From Proposition
2
and Corollary
3
we can deduce certain generic comparisons between the asymptotic convergence rates, depending on the compression operator (for compression operators having the same variance bound). They are proven in
Section

E.3
. In the following, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we use the notation
a\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\vbox to-0.60275pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vss}}}b
, to denote a
systematic inequality
(i.e.,
a\leq b
) with a negligible difference as
d\to\infty
(i.e.,
a=b+O(1/d)
), and similarly for any two symmetric matrices
A,B\in\mathcal{S}_{d}(\mathbb{R})
,
A\mathrel{\mathop{\preccurlyeq}\limits^{\vbox to-0.90417pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vss}}}B
, for
A\preccurlyeq B
and
A=B+O(1/d)
as
d\to\infty
.

Proposition 3

(Comparison between
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}}
,
\omega=d/h-1
)
.

We consider
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}
,
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}\}
with
p=h/d
, such that
\mathcal{C}
always satisfies
Lemma

1
with
\omega=d/h-1
. For any matrix
M\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
:

1.

If
M
is diagonal, then:

•

\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})=\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},p_{{M}})=\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},p_{{M}})=\frac{d}{h}M
,

•

\mathrm{Tr}\big(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}/\mathrm{s}/\mathrm{rd}h},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\big)\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)
.

This means that the asymptotic convergence rate does not depend on the choice of the compressor between
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1}
in the diagonal case.

2.

Moreover, for any matrix
M
with a
constant diagonal
(e.g., we standardize
6
6

6

That means we center and rescale to get a variance of one for each feature.
the data in the pre-processing step, such that
\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)=\mathrm{I}_{d}
), we have:

\mathrm{Tr}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})M^{-1})\leq\mathrm{Tr}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\Phi},p_{{M}})M^{-1})\leq\mathrm{Tr}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})M^{-1})\leq\mathrm{Tr}(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h},p_{{M}})M^{-1})\,.

With strict inequalities if
M
is not proportional to
\mathrm{I}_{d}
. This means that we expect the asymptotic convergence rate to be faster for PP than Sparsification, Sketching, or Rand-
h
(illustrated in experiments).

In the next proposition, we compare compressors
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}
for equal
\omega=\sqrt{d}
(we exclude
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
for which
h
must be an integer and that are shown to be worse than
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}
in
Proposition

3
).

Proposition 4

(Comparison between
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}}
,
\omega=\sqrt{d}
)
.

We consider
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}\}
with
p=(\sqrt{d}+1)^{-1}
, such that
\mathcal{C}
always satisfies
Lemma

1
with
\omega=\sqrt{d}
.

1.

For any symmetric matrix
M
diagonal, we have:

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)\overset{\text{possib. }\ll}{\leq}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},M)M^{-1}\right)\,.

2.

If
M
is not necessarily diagonal but with a
constant diagonal
(e.g., after standardization), then

•

\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},M)\preccurlyeq\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},p_{{M}})

•

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},M)M^{-1}\right)

This means that sparsification is expected to always result in a poorer asymptotic convergence rate than quantization. Moreover, the upper bound on the covariance
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},M)
for quantization itself leads to a worst bound than for PP.
7
7

7

Note that the behavior for quantization, apart from the upper bound
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},M)
is not quantified, it is thus possible that quantization performs even better than PP.

We now propose a detailed illustration of the results of
Proposition

2
and
Corollary

3
, first in a low-dimensional setting (
d=2
) and then in higher dimension on synthetic and real datasets.

– recall that
{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[x_{k}\right]}}\subset{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}(x_{k})\right]}}\Leftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}{\mathrm{Cov}\left[x_{k}\right]}}\preccurlyeq{\color[rgb]{1,0.4961,0.0547}{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}(x_{k})\right]}}
), as well as their two eigenvectors; we take

, hence for
\mathcal{C}\in\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{sq}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}\}
we have

but for sketching and rand-
1
, we have
p=1/2
and

.

We make the following observations:

[Qtz]

For quantization and stabilized quantization, in the non-diagonal case, the eigenvectors of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[x_{k}\right]}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}(x_{k})\right]}
are slightly
8
8

8

On the figure, there are nearly aligned, but actually differ.
different (as
\sqrt{\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)}
and
M
are not jointly diagonalizable, as well as if
\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)
is constant, although this case is not presented here, but in
Figure

S5
in
Section

E.3
). They are equal for the diagonal case (as
\sqrt{\mathrm{Diag}\left(M\right)}
and
M
are both diagonal so the eigenvectors are aligned with the axis). In both cases, the eigenvalue decay is reduced (from
\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}=1/10
without compression to
1/\sqrt{10}
with compression, which visually corresponds to a “wider” ellipse).

This slower eigenvalue decay results from the
unstructured-noise
, i.e., large noise on the weak-curvature direction, which is particularly visible on
Figure

4
. This is critical as it results in a potentially much larger limit rate, as
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{q},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)\simeq\mathrm{Tr}\left(M^{-1/2}\right)
.

[Skt]

For sketching, the eigenvectors remain the same for
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[x_{k}\right]}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}(x_{k})\right]}
(as
\mathrm{I}_{2}
and
M
are jointly diagonalizable, see
Corollary

3
), both in the diagonal and non-diagonal case. However, the isotropic noise with covariance
\mathrm{I}_{2}
is visible (wide ellipse), also drastically impacting
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)\varpropto\mathrm{Tr}\left(M^{-1}\right)
.

[Sp]

For
p
-sparsification, eigenvectors are not aligned with the ones of
M
in the non-diagonal case, but are in the diagonal case. In this latter case, the covariance
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})
is proportional to
M
.

[Rd]

Same remarks hold for Rand-
1
than for sparsification. We see that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1},p_{{M}})
is diagonal, as expected. Again, both operators induce an unstructured-noise in the non-diagonal case.

[PP]

For PP, the covariances are always proportional (with factor
p^{-1}
), i.e., the ellipses have the same axis and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}(x_{k})\right]}
is a scaled version of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{Cov}\left[x_{k}\right]}
.

We highlight the following points regarding pairwise comparisons:

•

In the diagonal case, as stated by
Item

1
in
Proposition

3
,
{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}(x_{k})\right]}
and
{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}(x_{k})\right]}
are identical.
{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1}(x_{k})\right]}
would have been identical too if
p=1/d
(but here we observe
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}1},p_{{M}})\preccurlyeq\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s/\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})
because the variance of rand-
1
is smaller that for sparsification/PP).

•

In the non-diagonal case, from
Item

2
in
Proposition

3
, we have
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)\leq\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})M^{-1}\right)
, however we do not have
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}},p_{{M}})\preccurlyeq\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})
, hence we can not conclude anything on
{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}(x_{k})\right]}
and
{\mathrm{Cov}\left[\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}(x_{k})\right]}
.

•

In the non-diagonal scenario, we observe on
Figure

3
, that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{q},p_{{M}})\preccurlyeq\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{C}_{s},p_{{M}})
(as in
Item

2
in

4
).

Interpretation.

(Top-row):
with standardization, the order predicted from

.

(large

), and

.

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h}\simeq\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
.
For quantization, we observe two regimes: 1) when

tends to zero, quantization and PP outperform sketching, sparsification, and rand-
h
, that are equivalent.
2) when

(Middle-row):
in the diagonal regime, comments made for

-l are still valid.

(Bottom-row):
We observe that for a generic matrix

(obtained from raw-data) there is no systematic order between compression schemes. This is un-avoidable as the order for a “
M

with constant-diagonal” is

•

\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{PP}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{s}}\mathrel{\mathop{\leq}\limits^{\vbox to-0.60275pt{\kern-2.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\vss}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{rd}h}\ll\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{q}}\ll\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}

.

We also observe that
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
, which is the only operator to always induce an isotropic component, may be much worse than all other compressors (e.g., on

). Ultimately, the order depends on the covariance matrix

. Here we observe that the raw-data behavior is close for

to the standardized version, while for

the order between compressors is the same for raw-data and diagonal (although the ratios are different). In

(

3.4
Numerical experiments on Algorithm
2

In this section, we run
2
on both synthetic and real datasets to illustrate the combined theoretical results of
Sections

2
and
3
.
In
Figure

7
, we compare the compression operators to the baseline of no-compression.
We plot the excess loss of the Polyak-Ruppert iterate
F(\overline{w}_{k})-F(w)
, versus the index in log/log scale. Each experiment is conducted 5 times, with a new dataset generated from a new seed. The standard deviation of
\log_{10}(F(\overline{w}_{k})-F(w))
is indicated by the shadow-area.

Setting:
(a)
Synthetic dataset generation:
The dataset is generated using
2
with
K=10^{7}
,
\sigma^{2}=1
, an optimal point
w_{*}
set as a constant vector of ones and a geometric eigenvalues decay of
D_{1}=\mathrm{Diag}\left((1/i^{4})_{i=1}^{d}\right)
(resp.
D_{2}=\mathrm{Diag}\left((1/i)_{i=1}^{d}\right)
).
For
i\in\{1,2\}
, the covariance matrix is
H_{\{i\}}=QD_{\{i\}}Q^{T}
, where
Q
is either orthogonal matrix, or
Q=\mathrm{I}_{d}
in the case of a diagonal features’ matrix.
(b)
Real datasets processing:
We resize images of the
cifar-10
dataset to a
16\times 16
dimension, and then for both datasets, we apply standardization. To compute the optimal point (and so to compute the excess loss), we run SGD over
200
passes on the whole dataset and consider the last Polyak-Ruppert average as the optimal point
w_{*}
.
(c)
2
:

We take a constant step-size
\gamma=1/(2(\omega+1)R^{2})
with
R^{2}
the trace of the features’ covariance,
and
w_{0}=0
as initial point. We set the batch-size
b=1
(resp.
b=16)
and the compressor variance
\omega=10
(resp.
\omega=1
, thus a factor 4 compression for
quantum
and factor 2 for
cifar-10
) for synthetic datasets (resp. for real datasets). For
cifar-10
and
quantum
, we run
2
for
5\times 10^{6}
iterations (it corresponds to
100
passes on the whole dataset). These settings are summarized in
Tables

S1
and
S2
in
Section

A.1
.
Additionally, to illustrate
Corollary

1
, we plot on
Figure

7(d)
the final excess loss after running
2
with an horizon-dependent step-size
\gamma=K^{-2/5}
, computed for seven values of
K\in\{10^{i},i\in\llbracket 1,7\rrbracket\}
.

Interpretation –
H
diagonal
(
Figure

7(a)
).
For sparsification, rand-
h
, and PP (linear compressors), the rate of convergence is given by
Theorem

2
. As stated by
Corollary

3
, the covariance
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}
is proportional to
H
leading to a
O(1/K)
rate. We indeed observe in
Figure

7(a)
that excess loss is linear in a log/log scale.

For non-linear compression operators, the rate is given by
Theorem

1
.
On the one hand,
1
-quantization results in a slower eigenvalues’ decay, leading to a larger
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H^{-1}\right)
, thus a slower convergence than linear compressors. On the other hand, for sketching, covariance has a purely isotropic part scaling with
\mathrm{I}_{d}
, which causes
\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{ania}}H^{-1}\right)
to strongly depend on the strong-convexity coefficient
\mu
resulting in an extremely large constant. Both behaviors are observed in
Figure

7(a)
.

Interpretation –
H
not diagonal
(
Figures

7(b)
and
7(e)
). In the case of the high eigenvalues’ decay of
H_{1}
(
\mu=10^{-8}
), the only compressor that shows in
Figure

7(b)
a linear rate of convergence in the log/log scale is PP.
All others exhibit a saturation phenomenon after a certain number of iterations. This is again due to the unstructured part of the noise for all other compressors, as given by
Proposition

2
.
Besides, we also note an increase of the excess loss after some iterations that is likely caused by the accumulation of noise on axis onto which the curvature of
H
is weak (but the isotropic noise is not).
However, taking the optimal horizon-dependent step-size given by
Corollary

1
, we recover on
Figure

7(d)
for all compressor
\mathcal{C}
the sub-linear convergence rate of PP shown at
Figure

7(b)
, reducing by a factor
100
the excess loss w.r.t. to the scenario where
\gamma=1/2(\omega+1)R^{2})
. While using a small step-size is slightly worse for SGD, it reduces the second and third term of the variance in
Theorem

1
that depends on
\mu
for other compressors.
And in the scenario of a slow eigenvalues’ decay (
\mu=10^{-2}
), we observe on
Figure

7(e)
that all compressors reach the sub-linear rate (same slope -1 on the log/log plot), but with different constants. This illustrates
Theorems

2
and
1
in the case of moderate coefficient
\mu
where we expect the second and third parts of the variance term to be negligible.

Synthetic dataset

Real datasets

(a)

diagonal.

(b)

H_{1}
not diagonal.

(c)

not diagonal,

.
